<meta>This page continues a dialogue between the figure of Mercury and a Smelter. The Smelter is speaking at the top of the page, continuing his thought from the previous page about the danger of revealing spiritual-chemical truths that challenge orthodox views.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>* o *</header>

...demonstrated, namely that all created things here below must be brought back to their **first essence** <term>first essence: "erstes Wesen," referring to the pure, original state of matter before it was corrupted by the "fall" or earthly elements</term> and transformed into a divine or spiritual quality; and if I further showed the means and ways by which this should happen, I would be considered an **arch-heretic** <term>arch-heretic: "Erz-Ketzer," a supreme or notorious heretic who leads others into false doctrine</term>; for I know that ears are already prickling when even the slightest thought is given to it.

*Mercurius.* There are indeed people who are filled with all-too-premature judgment, but who have heard nothing yet of the true quality of such things. But if the knot were untied for them, and all these natural occurrences were fully revealed, they might yet receive a light from such great effects. Without this, it remains a deeply hidden **mystery** <term>mystery: "Geheimniß" (modern: Geheimnis), often used in alchemical texts to refer to the "Arcanum" or hidden workings of God in nature</term> to them, despite the fact that everything is true and cannot be suppressed by any human darkening. For God (which we should finally see and notice) eventually reveals such mysteries; He daily carries forward this hidden **glorification** <term>glorification: "Verherrlichung," the process by which a base substance or "creature" is purified and raised to a higher, more spiritual state</term> within His creatures that lie among the corrupting earthly things. He makes a beginning in them already at their birth, because they are subject to temporal death, which is, as it were, the end of their earthly corruption. Only after this dissolution can glorification follow in them; for this dissolution all creatures anxiously long, and they do not rest until, after this course of time, they are released from their burdensome earthly load and have reached their **first essence**.

<meta>catchword: Schmel- (Note: This indicates the next speaker is the Schmelzer, or Smelter)</meta>

*The Smelter.* Daily experience teaches us that the workings of Nature (which she constantly drives here below according to the all-wise governance of God) cannot all be researched and fathomed until one has attained perfect knowledge of them here; nevertheless, it is possible through God’s grace to reach very far and see into the heart—that is, the innermost part—of Nature (or these secret divine beings), which is almost unbelievable to the ignorant. Therefore, humans are permitted to know much, if they only apply themselves properly to exploring it.

*Mercury.* When creatures have thus completed their course of time and ended their temporal life in the spirit of their beginning, whereby their spirit is taken from them once more, this **sidereal spirit** <term>sidereal spirit: "Syderische Geist," the celestial or "star-derived" influence that animates physical bodies and governs their growth</term> can perform nothing further upon them. Instead, it has reached the end of its influence (from beginning to end) with them. Through this, they are indeed released from their burdensome bodily weights, destroyed in their corporeal being, and completely reduced to ashes. This prepares them to undergo a full **dissolution** <term>dissolution: "Dissolvirung," the chemical and spiritual process of breaking down a substance into its fundamental parts to prepare it for transformation</term>. However, this (here tempered) great **universal world-spirit** <term>universal world-spirit: "universal Welt-Geist," also known as the *Spiritus Mundi*, a subtle, life-giving essence believed to permeate the entire universe</term> cannot accomplish this within them, because it is only capable of mixing with the living earthly exhalations here to awaken them to life and to separate life from them; but with the dead earth, called **damned earth** <note>original: "Terra damnata," the useless, scorched residue left over after an alchemical distillation or intense heating</note>, it has nothing—

<summary>The Smelter and Mercury discuss the limits of human understanding concerning Nature’s inner workings. Mercury explains that once a creature dies and its "sidereal spirit" departs, the physical body must be reduced to ashes to undergo total dissolution, a task the general world-spirit cannot perform on dead matter.</summary>

**Smelter.** This is exactly what I have been so eager to know, and what I cannot yet grasp for myself: namely, how it stands with the human spirit, body, and soul, and where they go after death? For some say one goes straight to Heaven or Hell, but others say one must first go into Purgatory <note>original: "Fege-Feuer"</note>, which I cannot yet rightly reconcile within myself.

**Mercury.** As much as belongs to humans regarding this earthliness or the **Animal Kingdom** <term>Animal Kingdom: original Latin "Regnum Animale"; in alchemical philosophy, this refers to the realm of living, sentient creatures, specifically the physical and organic functions of the human body</term>—that is, according to the body—I will hereby faithfully and fully reveal to you: namely, how such a body may be brought to its pure essence. However, how it shall go with him according to the soul, and where he arrives after this temporal life, every person should, by right, be able to perceive for themselves through their own conviction. You know the laws and rights of **GOD** from His word, and how you should live according to them; do that, and you will be able to escape the future wrath of **GOD**.

**Smelter.** Because you believe that what I desire to know from you might be too high for me, and is not necessary for the knowledge of nature and the creature, we shall hereby pass over it. Yet I still ask this of you: to fully explain the manner and way of the **dissolution** <term>dissolution: "Auflösung," the process of breaking a substance down into its primary components, often the first step in alchemical transformation</term> and **restoration** <term>restoration: "Wiederbringung," the alchemical or spiritual act of returning a thing to its perfected, incorruptible state</term> of all dead things. Let us return to our **mountain-discourse** <note>original: "Berg-Discours"; likely referring to their previous discussion regarding mining, metals, and the "mountains" where minerals are formed</note>, to spend the rest of our time this night upon it, so that I may still profit somewhat from you.

This brown gold-talc you mean is also very well known to me; it breaks out in a white crystalline quartz in a rock on the surface. The vein where it stands is beneath the rocks, where a hole goes in; it is somewhat hidden and very narrow, and several medium-sized fir trees stand close by. This place is known to several other people who, as I know, take it away and subsequently make use of it, as that same ore is very rich in gold. Through roasting <term>Rösten; the process of heating ore to remove impurities like sulfur before smelting</term> you must compel it, so that the still very volatile spirits and the delicate metal of the same may fix <term>figiren; an alchemical term for making a volatile substance stable and solid so it does not evaporate</term> themselves; then you may obtain your yield. On the broad stone found there, roasting and smelting has been done for a long time, as you yourself will be able to deduce if you have seen such; there also stands a stone erected below, not far from such a roasting-place, as a monument the height of a man, on which also an Ox-Head (however)

as you can well imagine, I suffered hunger and thirst, and many times could not reach any inn; yet it was always in vain and for nothing. On such mountains I have encountered very many garnets <note>original: *Granaten*; common semi-precious silicate minerals often found in metamorphic rocks</note> and grains, which were partly in stones, and partly in sand and clay <term>Letten; a miner's term for a tough, fatty clay or loam often found within mineral veins</term>; likewise various types of marcasites <note>original: *Marcasitten*; iron sulfide minerals often confused with gold due to their metallic luster, popularly known as "fool's gold"</note> of white, gray, brown, and yellow color, which often looked like native gold, but I was unable to obtain any metal from them in any way, no matter what I did. I collected a considerable quantity of these and later gave them away to good friends. I searched all the waters and rivers, wells and ponds on the mountains, seeking to wash the famous gold from them; while I did encounter various glittering little stones in them, I could obtain nothing from them.

**Mercurius.** Now, you have certainly gone to great trouble to search the mountains and to discover rich gold-bearing ores upon them. This is truly a most useful pursuit, for it can encourage one toward a deeper contemplation of nature and, through such investigations, eventually lead to her secret paths. nature is formed thus, and found in such mountain ranges in great quantity. ...will bring to light very rich **gold ores**, in which many a person has made his fortune and gained great benefit. Others, by contrast, must look at them in vain—just as happened to you previously—and must suffer having them taken from under their very noses and smelted, because they themselves understand nothing.

**Smelter.** When I was on this mountain range for the first time in the year 1739 <note>original: *Anno 1739*</note> in the month of June, to look around there as a miner, and arrived by chance with a good friend at the aforementioned rock at the **Ox-Head** <note>original: *Ochsen-Kopff*; a prominent peak in the Fichtelgebirge mountains of Bavaria</note>, a misfortune occurred: on the so-called **Snow Mountain** <note>original: *Schneeberge*; the Schneeberg is the highest peak in the Fichtelgebirge</note> (which lies directly opposite), a great conflagration arose from negligence. Thereby great damage was done to the timber, as a great deal of felled and still-standing wood was burned; despite the fact that in a short time a very large number of people were present to combat such an evil, it nevertheless burned for many days, which I was able to see and recognize very well from that rock. *Mercurius.* The gold found in very many places among us in rivers and lakes truly grows within them in some places, and is generated and born therein contrary to all human reason; this is certainly a quite different and special generation than that which is performed in the earth. This secret **Metallic Birth** <term>Metallische Geburth: The early modern belief that metals "grow" like plants within the earth or water, maturing from "imperfect" metals into "perfect" ones like gold.</term> can then give rise to many profound matters, and there lies more hidden beneath it than many imagine. However, there are also rivers and streams that carry such gold because it is washed into them by waters springing from rich mountains; in these cases, such waters—flowing from rich, solid, and naturally grown gold veins—tear away and wash down small gold nuggets <note>original: "Gold-Stiefgen," referring to small mineral specimens or pieces of ore.</note> or gold flakes <note>original: "Gold-Flammen," literally 'gold-flames,' describing thin, bright scales of gold.</note> and bring them into other rivers and streams through their rapid flow, where they are subsequently found and revealed. For it is certain that the rivers and streams running through great mountains commonly carry gold with them, which so often stems from the runoff of such rich gold-mountains, through which one can often recognize the internal mineral content of such rich mountains.

*Schmeltzer.* But how does it happen that in flowing water, among so much sand, solid pure gold can grow, and by what means is nature able to perform this? For in such water, it has no solid, enclosed stone for such ripening, and consequently no suitable **matrix** <term>Matrix: The "womb" or surrounding rock material in which a mineral or metal is formed and held.</term> as it has in the earth, wherein it might grow and [become] cor-

<sig>L 5</sig>

...physical <note>The text completes the word "corporalisch" (corporeal/physical) from the previous page.</note>? I cannot yet comprehend such generation and growth.

**Mercury.** Gold is not the only thing found grown in the rivers and streams that flow through large mountains; one also finds silver, copper, iron, tin, and lead. Indeed, one often finds "living" mercury <term>lebendiger Mercurius: Native quicksilver found in its liquid form in nature, considered by alchemists to be the "seed" of all metals.</term>, cinnabar, orpiment <term>Aurum pigment: Arsenic trisulfide, a bright yellow mineral once used as a pigment and thought to be a precursor to gold.</term>, and antimony along with them. As I have mentioned, these are often washed away from the veins and lodes of the earth, and they may be rich or poor in content, fixed or volatile, depending on how those veins were formed.

However, such minerals are also frequently generated in the waters by the workings of nature, just as they are in the earth. This occurs especially where there are rich mountains, which are frequently filled with mineral vapors and fumes of the earth. Their strong "exhalations" <note>original: "Witterung," a term in early mining referring to the "scent" or subtle vapors released by ore veins that were believed to guide prospectors.</note> or evaporations rise strongly there and pass through such waters, rivers, or stagnant lakes. These vapors then mix with the mercury that pours down to us—the "first beginner" of all created things—retaining it. Through the influence of the celestial astral powers, they coagulate <term>coaguliren: In alchemical theory, the process of a spiritual or vaporous substance taking on a solid form.</term> within these waters. They then mature into a fixed metallic or mineral body, according to whether the rising earth-vapors are pure or earthy; just as I have perfectly and clearly taught you regarding the generation of the metals and minerals within the earth. Through such workings, we find in both flowing and stagnant waters (just as in the earth) so many kinds of metals and minerals.

*Smelter.* Now another question occurs to me, which Nature, with her astonishing effects, drives me to understand thoroughly. In such rivers in large and rich mountain ranges, I have encountered many wonderful, rare, and noble stones among the wild pebbles and sand; such as actual topazes, hyacinths <term>Hyacinths: In historical mineralogy, this term usually referred to transparent orange-red or brownish-red stones, often varieties of zircon or garnet.</term>, sapphires, amethysts, crystals, garnets, jaspers, and other such things. I cannot quite comprehend how, given the size of some of them, they could have been carried from the veins <note>original: "Gängen," referring to ore lodes or mineral veins in the rock.</note> of the earth by water; much less how they could have become hard, solid, and transparent bodies in such waters. For I have found amethysts and jasper in such places weighing half a pound; stones which surely could not have been carried away from the veins by such subtle <note>In this context, "subtle" refers to water that is thin, refined, or highly filtered, which the Smelter believes would lack the force to move heavy rocks.</note> spring water, nor brought so far from them. Both of these things seem very difficult to believe, even though nature is capable of doing and performing unshakeably <note>original: "unschwencklich," meaning steadfastly or immeasurably.</note> much more than we can understand or grasp.

*Mercury.* Let this no longer seem so strange to you, my Smelter, for I have already presented the generation and birth of these stones, as well as all other things, quite thoroughly and clearly; from which you might well see and understand that such special workings of nature are quite possible. For these stones you have just mentioned, both noble and base, are all generated <term>generiret: From the Latin "generare," meaning to be produced, birthed, or brought into existence through natural processes.</term> daily by nature according to their properties, both in water and in the earth, and there is born...

*Mercury.* Because you cannot yet grasp that the rocks, stones, or cliffs of the mountains still grow daily even now, because one cannot easily see or feel such a thing in them—since it is hardly noticeable in a hundred years, or perhaps in such a time, regarding their enlargement, just as much is broken off from them or devastated by time—yet you also do not know how large a rock (which you may know well) was a hundred or more years ago, since you did not see it then, and your descendants in a hundred years will not see it as it is now. However, besides the workings of nature, which you see every day and which lie constantly before your eyes in all three kingdoms <note>The Mineral, Vegetable, and Animal kingdoms</note>, I want to point you to those old mining structures <note>original: "Stollen-Gebäude"; refers to the tunnels and galleries of a mine</note> which are driven into the solid rock. Without doubt, at their first beginning at the tunnel mouth <note>original: "Mundloch"; the entrance or adit of a mine</note>, they must have been wide enough so that the walls and mountains lying nearby—which often make up a very large spoil heap <note>original: "Halte" (Halde); the pile of waste rock accumulated outside a mine</note>—could be hauled and brought out through them. These, however, have now become so overgrown <note>The author uses "verwachsen" to imply the rock itself has expanded to fill the void</note> and so narrow that you are no longer able to travel into them, no matter how you might try; you will encounter very many such examples if you only pay attention to such things. Even if it has been a period of 6 to 800 years since they were worked, you can still see enough from this that during the time since those buildings became abandoned <note>original: "aufläßig"; a mining term for a mine that is no longer in operation</note>, the tunnels grew shut again, so that in many places it looks only like a crack or a stone parting <note>original: "Steinscheidung"; a natural joint or seam in the rock where two layers meet</note>. **Smelter.** Now I recognize the true reason for it, and I see that the proportions of these bodies in all three kingdoms <note>The Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral kingdoms</note>—in their life, growth, and demise—are of one and the same kind, just as they flowed from one root and were prepared from one <term>Materia</term> <note>original: "Materia"; the fundamental matter or substance of the universe</note> at the beginning. Even though they are very different in their movements, forms, and shapes, the beginning of every thing is equal to its end, and the end returns to that matter which it was in the beginning; just as Nature, as their master craftsman, daily makes us recognize through her high workings, provided one only pays attention and wishes to be guided by them. But according to our blind reason, these remain locked mysteries to us.

**Mercury.** Yes, he who does not study the school of Nature well, and has not thoroughly discovered all of her hidden secrets, can [do nothing] in the Mineral

**Smelter.** When I consider all of this, which I have heard one after another in these several hours regarding the astonishing workings of nature, I am almost struck with wonder; indeed, my heart beats within my body in full surges. ...been moved to tear you out of this confused error, and thus to lead you that you may reach the true, hidden paths of **active nature** <term>active nature (würckenden Natur): in alchemy, this refers to nature's inherent power to transform and grow matter</term>. Therefore I ask you only this: do not despise her lowly ways, and do not strive after grand and different things, and do not follow your own will and understanding in this matter, but solely and alone my sincere teachings; they will certainly reward you one day. For nature loves only her own kind; therefore, if you add something repugnant and unbearable to her, you cannot hope for anything fruitful from it.

**Smelter.** I have very well considered your speeches until now, and have grasped the clear concept from them all, so that I now see quite well that this is the right way to fulfill my desire and thereby to finish my work; also that all my **fire-operations** <term>fire-operations (Feuer-Arbeiten): the traditional high-heat smelting techniques the Smelter used previously</term> carried out until now are a useless endeavor, through which nothing good could ever have been established, but rather I spoil more than I make good. Therefore, just continue further; your teaching and instruction will not be in vain upon me.

**Mercurius.** Listen: when you obtain an ore that is very volatile, and additionally very rich in subtle gold and silver, you may first have it crushed to flour (or, according to the circumstances, even processed into **silt** <term>silt (Schlich): the fine, muddy residue left after ore is crushed and washed</term> and washed), and then put it into a large wooden vessel made for that purpose, and successively pour your **salt solution** <note>original: "Solutionem Salis"</note> (whichever you find to be good) upon it, and stir it well together with a shovel,

...and let it stand for several days, then have it roasted and fixed <term>fixed (figiren): an alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable so it can withstand heat without evaporating</term> according to nature's way in a specifically prepared fixing-furnace <term>fixing-furnace (Figir-Ofen): a specialized oven used in early modern chemistry to stabilize substances</term>, well-sealed or covered. You will produce much gold and silver, and thereby bring joy to your mining company <term>mining company (Gewerckschaft): a historical legal form of a mining consortium or partnership</term>. Once you have become a master of this work and have it in daily practice, you will then be capable of treating <note>original: "tractiren"</note> your ores in various ways and manners, and one labor will guide you to the next, such that you will marvel at it.

**Smelter.** This is truly a secret way of roasting and smelting that I have never heard or seen before. I well believe that something extraordinary may be accomplished with it. I rejoice in this from my heart; the country and its people shall benefit from it, and what you have revealed to me here so far, I will, if it pleases God, soon put into practice. But tell me, after their roasting, can I not also immediately thrust the poor and low-grade ores into the lead <note>original: "ins Bley stechen" refers to the technique of adding ore directly to a bath of molten lead</note>?

*Mercurius.* In that case, you must first observe whether you can increase their content through roasting, and whether you might be able to dispense with matte smelting <term>matte smelting (Steinschmelzen): a process of melting sulfide ores to produce a "matte" or "stone," a concentrated mixture of metal sulfides</term>. You must also consider what metals your ores consist of—whether there is also much copper present alongside the gold and silver. For if the matte otherwise becomes too rich, it will not allow itself to be easily treated with lead, and the silver will not willingly separate from it through liquation <term>liquation (seigern): a metallurgical process where metals are separated by heating them to a temperature where one melts and flows away while the other remains solid</term>; instead, it "robs" <note>original: "raubet," referring to metal being lost into the slag</note> and stalls in the furnace. However, if there is much copper among it, you must smelt it into a matte in order to obtain the copper as well; for such matte is then treated with lead and the silver liquated from it, as will be quite well known to you.

*Smelter.* What do you think generally about the washing and the drawing of concentrate <term>concentrate (Schlich): the fine, muddy ore residue left after crushing and washing away the lighter waste rock</term> from the ores? Does not the most subtle and volatile (and therefore perhaps the greatest part of) the metal often depart during such work? Could such a process be beneficial to delicate ores—especially those that contain gold? I still have some nagging doubts <note>original: "Grillen," literally "crickets," meaning whims or persistent worries</note> about this.

*Mercurius.* The lesser ores, regardless of what kind of rock or metallic property they consist of, need only be broken into pieces (about the size of nuts) and then committed to roasting. The rich ores and those containing subtle metals, however, should only be stamped <term>stamped (gepochet): crushed in a mill using heavy vertical hammers</term>, and depending on the results of the assay, they should often not be turned into concentrate at all. This is unless the ores are coarse and yield a stable concentrate, or if one had them in great quantity; in those cases, I would allow it. For otherwise, the volatile matter—which often still fixes itself in the fire—will pass through <unclear>it</unclear>...

notice that the blende-like <term>blende-like (blendigen): deceptive minerals like zinc blende (sphalerite) that were often mistaken for valuable galena but were harder to process</term> ores and mineral types require quite a special treatment <note>original: *Tractament*</note> in the fire during roasting and smelting, compared to perhaps the horn-like and pyritic types, because their metallic content in such cases is very subtle and volatile, and also had a much different matrix <term>matrix (Matrice): the surrounding rock or "mother" material in which a mineral or crystal is embedded</term> than the same had during its generation; even if they were mined together or had stood right next to each other. Even if they have succeeded so far as to "force" their ores to some extent and thereby obtain some content from them, this is still far from the path by which they would be able to achieve their purpose and bring the ores to "obedience"—namely, so that the subtle and volatile golden essence within them might be ripened and <term>fixed</term> <note>original: "figiret"; an alchemical term for making a volatile or "spirituous" substance stable so it can withstand the heat of the furnace without evaporating.</note> (just as I have now taught you through your art).

**Mercurius.** In **Styria** <note>original: "Steyermarck"; a mountainous region in modern-day Austria, famous since antiquity for its high-quality "Norick" iron.</note> there are also very beautiful mountain ranges, which contain very many—but also still **volatile—gold-bearing ores** and types of rock. These, however, cannot easily be smelted because of their great instability. Otherwise, these mountains have made themselves very well known in mining because of the many good **ironstones** <note>original: "Eisensteine"; a historical term for iron ore.</note> found there; this ironstone is of such exceptional quality that they smelt from it the most beautiful and best iron, which far surpasses all other iron in character and property. It can almost be called a perfectly good **steel**, as it possesses uncommon hardness and extraordinary **malleability** <note>original: "Geschmeidigkeit"; the ability of the metal to be shaped or hammered without breaking.</note>, so that very useful instruments and tools are manufactured from it, which are exported to almost all parts of the world and used to great advantage, as will be quite well known to you.

**Smelter.** That they smelt a great deal of iron in Styria is indeed very well known to me, from which it can certainly be concluded that they must maintain a strong **mining operation** <note>original: "Berg-Bau".</note> there, and may also encounter various types of ores that, upon proper examination, could yield a **gold content**. But because such an art is now so rare and strange, and is known in so few places, they will likely not achieve much with those ores because of their heavy **volatility** <note>referring to "fugitive" metals that evaporate or vanish as smoke during heating if not properly "fixed."</note>; for it is also not yet known that they smelt any gold from their ores there. Indeed, in **Germany**, it is a great art to smelt gold, because very special ores are required for it.

The world is also constantly kept in this unhappy sleep and slumber of ignorance by great, boastful, and flattering endearments, and thus always chooses the most bitter and tasteless shells over the loveliest, sweetest kernels.

However, it is as you have just said: the people of **Styria** <note>a mountainous, ore-rich region in modern-day Austria</note>, alongside their many iron-smelteries, could likely also smelt enough gold and silver if they only knew how to arrange it properly. Thus, alongside such highly necessary ironworks, they could also pursue the same, and not forget the one for the sake of the other. But because such high science has become a secret in all places, it is very difficult to find it again.

**Mercury.** In Styria there is, among other volatile ores and types of mountain minerals, a pyritic ore <note>original: *kiesiges Erz*</note> that they call **Sugarloaf** <note>original: *Zuckenhut*; likely named for its conical crystalline shape</note>. It is so ***sulfurous*** and ***arsenical*** (according to their way of speaking) that it cannot be roasted or smelted, but rather flies entirely away in the fire, body and soul. Yet they have discovered such a clever technique <note>original: *Kunstgriff*</note> for it, that they may obtain a golden content from this pyrite, and it consists of this: They pound the pyrite small into a flour, and boil it in a **potash lye** <term>potash lye: an alkaline solution made from wood ashes, used here to chemically "fix" or clean the ore</term> and a **willow-ash lye** until it becomes quite thick. They repeat this several times; then they boil it down into a stone and, during smelting, add some silver to it (so that the still-volatile and subtle metal may receive a **hold** <note>original: *Anhalt*; in metallurgy, a "collector" metal like silver or lead is added to capture vaporous precious metals before they escape as fumes</note>). The **lead-work** <note>original: *Bleywerck*; the lead used in the cupellation process to separate gold and silver</note> from this, they then refine—

<summary>The dialogue discusses the loss of metallurgical knowledge and describes a specific Styrian process for extracting gold from volatile, "Sugarloaf" pyritic ores using alkaline lyes and silver as a fixing agent.</summary>
<keywords>Styria, metallurgy, gold extraction, pyrite, Sugarloaf ore, potash lye, arsenic, silver collector, smelting secrets</keywords>

**Smelter.** If the Styrians obtain gold in such a manner from their **Sugarloaf** or **arsenical volatile gold-pyrites**, then surely something extraordinary could be achieved and won with my inverted or **unlocked** <note>original: *aufgeschlossenen*; in alchemy, this refers to making a substance soluble or chemically reactive</note> **alkaline salt** <term>Sall alcali: an alkaline salt, such as potassium carbonate, used to neutralize acids</term>, or **potash lyes** (just as you have taught me)? Certainly, this same lye would provide the proper "bath" in which to pickle and **fix** <term>fix: to treat a volatile substance so that it remains stable in heat and does not evaporate</term> such pyrites, so that a very rich golden content might be obtained from them. I perceive the path you wish to show me; certainly, if these people rightly considered their work and reflected a little further on the process, they might soon fully achieve the true aim of their desire. However, as long as the harsh **acidity** <note>original: *Säuer-*; referring to the sulfurous or acidic components that hinder the process</note> in their salts is not **precipitated** <term>precipitated: causing a substance to settle out of a solution; here, it refers to neutralizing or removing impurities</term>, they cannot improve anything further through such means.

**Mercury.** It is for this very reason that I told you to meditate further on the matter, so that you might weigh my **salt solutions** <note>original: *Solutiones Salis*</note> against those others; then you will find the difference between them and easily grasp the benefit that will manifest in such works. The so-called **foundry smoke** <note>original: *Hütten Rauch*; the powdery, volatile arsenic trioxide that collects in the chimneys of smelting houses</note> or **"Hütterich" ore** requires exactly the same **treatment** <note>original: *Tractament*</note>, as these are so volatile and arsenical that they cannot be easily smelted without prior fixation. These

I have seen **Hütterich ores** <term>Hütterich-Erze: a historical term for volatile, arsenical pyrites or "arsenic-bloom" ores that were difficult to process</term> boiled only in the common way in a good **lye** for two days and nights, until it no longer foamed up; then they cast such ore into a barrel with water and let it stand overnight, and poured fresh lye over it again, and then let it boil down completely. Then they melted it into lead or silver and **cupelled** <term>abgetrieben: the process of refining gold or silver by melting it with lead in a cupel furnace</term> it, and thus they obtained a rich gold content through the **parting** <note>original: *Scheidung*; the chemical separation of gold from silver, usually using nitric acid</note>. And in this manner, gold and silver are melted from such volatile ores in the **Tauern** <note>original: *Taus*; likely referring to the Tauern mountain range in Austria, a major historical gold-mining region</note>. Now, the better your **charge** <term>Beschickung: the mixture of ore and additives prepared for the furnace</term> is when roasting such ores (which works in both the dry and wet ways), the richer the content you can then expect from them through the smelting.

**Smelter.** These are indeed such preparations and **fixations** <term>Figirung: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</term> from which one can see that these people have sought to lend Nature the necessary assistance in her operation. Although they have not yet completely achieved their perfect purpose through this, their natural seeking and inventing has nevertheless borne as much fruit as their powers were capable of producing. This should rightfully have encouraged them to further reflection, to investigate the secret ways of Nature more thoroughly and thereby fully discover them; by which they would be able to replace in their volatile ores that which they naturally lack.

**Mercury.** My dear Smelter, just have patience; it will reveal itself, that one [sees] many...

Such ores might be all the more easily **fixed** <term>figiren: an alchemical term for making a volatile substance stable so it does not evaporate</term>, especially since they already possess a foundation of native, fixed metal. However, with **amalgamation** <term>Anquicken: the process of combining ore with mercury</term> alone, if the ores are not very rich, nothing is accomplished, since such work neither **fixes** nor **ripens** <note>original: *zeitiget*; refers to the alchemical belief that metals 'grow' and mature in the earth</note> anything, but only keeps for itself through the mercury that which is already complete metal. It is indeed a fine work, but it requires such ores as are very rare and not found in great abundance.

Thus I have shown you how the light may break through all darkness, whereby you may travel on your paths without standing still, until you might arrive at the place where you shall fulfill your desire. I have also mostly told you of the preparation and the burdening <term>Beschickung: the technical process of mixing ores with specific additives or fluxes in the correct proportions before they are put into the furnace</term> of the gold smelting currently practiced in our German lands, and how these are presently performed—even if I might have passed over several places (where gold is still smelted today) and, out of haste, did not indicate them to you, such as in Clausthal <note>original: *Constahl*; a famous mining town in the Harz mountains</note>, Goldkronach <note>original: *Gold-Kranach*; a historic gold-mining town in Bavaria</note>, and other places besides. Since, as already stated, such ores are not so rare, know that this was done because in such works—both in the roasting <note>original: *Rösten*; the process of heating ore to a high temperature to burn off impurities like sulfur without melting the metal</note> and the burdening of the ores—nothing artistic, much less natural, is performed. Indeed, I shudder to speak of such confused and destructive fire-works <note>original: *Feuer-Arbeiten*; referring to the brutal heat of common industrial furnaces</note>, and to think much upon them is a true vexation; for these reasons, I shall stay no longer upon them.

**Smelter.** Those must surely be among the best gold ores in our lands, since in smelting according to the common manner they already give a noticeable and profitable yield. If one were to work those ores according to Maturation <term>Maturation: the alchemical belief that metals "grow" and ripen in the earth; here, it refers to a process of accelerating that growth to turn base ore into gold</term>, what riches might be achieved! Therefore, such ores are rightly to be preferred over those which, due to great volatility <note>i.e., the metal evaporates or is lost as gas during the heat of the furnace</note>, yield no metal. It is a pity that they are processed so poorly, and not treated according to their proper burdenings and their specific characteristics.

*Mercurius.* Yes, yes, my good man, it is a pity for many an ore <term>Ertz: raw mineral or metal-bearing rock</term> that is sometimes destroyed in such a careless and thoughtless manner in your worthless roasting and smelting works, and driven away as a smoke and vapor <note>Refers to the loss of volatile metals and "spirits" that evaporate during high-heat processing before they can be captured.</note>, from which you might have obtained and received the most magnificent and richest profit; indeed, [what was lost] yielded more than that which remained behind after its burning and roasting. However, your smelters and foundry workers will hardly believe me in this, because, according to their general custom, they do not easily accept anything more than what they themselves understand and grasp—as you also well know, since by your own confession you were also one of these unbelievers, and at the beginning you resisted my well-founded natural sciences <note>original: *Wissenschafften*; here meaning the systematic study of nature and alchemy</note> quite a bit, until you finally saw that such [common methods] could not hold water, but that I must be right, because you learned to see more clearly. For if you gentlemen only properly examined your works and truly took to heart the circumstances of the ores, you would see and recognize that such methods are fundamentally false and entirely against nature. Though you might not be able to change them immediately at the start as would be required for this, you can still find cause to reflect further upon such secrets, until you finally find the ground upon which everything rests, and wherein the art reaches its end—namely, that upon which everything depends in the roasting and smelting of your ores, which are the hidden paths of nature.

<header>* o *</header>

*Smelter.* How do things stand in Electoral Saxony <term>Chur-Sachsen: the territory ruled by the Elector of Saxony, a powerhouse of early modern mining</term> with their ore smelting? I have heard that there in Freiberg (the main mining town) all the ores of the entire country are brought together and processed in the smelting and foundry works located there. Are there no smelting foundries in the Upper Mountains <note>The *Ober-Gebürgen* refers to the Ore Mountains or Erzgebirge, the rugged highland region bordering Bohemia</note> so that they must transport the ore so far, or why is the system set up that way?

*Mercurius.* In the lands of Electoral Saxony, there is a very special arrangement regarding their ore smelting; for there, they have the so-called "Ore Purchase" <term>Ertz-Kauff: a legal system where the state held a monopoly on buying silver ore</term>. The richest silver ores from all their mountains are sent to the General Smelting Administration there, where the high Sovereign <note>The Elector of Saxony</note> purchases them for himself. After the common assays <term>Proben: chemical tests used to determine the metal content of a small sample</term> have been made, he pays in cash according to the weights in loths and marks <note>traditional units of weight; a Mark was roughly 233 grams, divided into 16 Loths</note>; then he has them smelted and refined <note>original: *abtreiben*; the process of cupellation to separate silver from lead</note> and has the silver minted into coins.

*Smelter.* So in those mountains, the mining companies <term>Gewerckschafften: cooperative associations of shareholders who funded and operated mines</term> are not permitted to smelt and process the ores they have won <note>original: *gewonnente*; meaning extracted or mined</note> themselves, but must transport them to Freiberg? There must be a particular reason behind that, as there is surely no lack of wood and charcoal in those mountains (which are likely more available there than around Freiberg), unless no more foundries could be established there, which would truly surprise me.

<header>* o *</header>

*Mercurius.* My dear Smelter, this is the reason: around the Freiberg district <note>original: *Zuge*, referring to a series of mining veins or a specific mining area</note>, many works are being mined that are very poor in ore or rich content, yet they occasionally become much richer (as has often happened there). These ores mostly consist of a galena <term>Bley-Glantze: lead glance or galena, the primary ore of lead, often containing silver</term> or sulfur-pyrite <term>Schweffel-Kieß: iron pyrite</term>. Sometimes a centner <note>a traditional unit of weight, roughly 110 pounds</note> of this material yields only 1 to 1 1/2 loth <note>about 1/2 to 3/4 of an ounce</note> of silver. On their own, such ores would not be worth the effort of smelting, as the labor and costs involved would not be repaid.

However, these poor ores can be added to the rich silver ores from the mountains (which are naturally more stubborn and "wild" <note>refractory ores that are difficult to melt on their own</note>) to serve as a good and necessary flux <note>material added to the furnace to help the ore melt and flow</note>. In this way, their small silver content can also be extracted—without additional cost—whereas otherwise, because of their poverty, they would have to be left lying abandoned. 

Because of this, as I have already told you, it has often been observed that such veins eventually become enriched, turning into very profitable mining operations. To benefit the mining companies <term>Gewerckschafften: cooperative associations of shareholders</term> and to ensure the continued existence and preservation of such works, the high Sovereign <note>The Elector of Saxony</note> was moved to introduce the "Ore Purchase" <term>Erz-Kauff</term>. As you can now well understand, the Sovereign gains no further profit from this particular arrangement. The pyritic ores serve the other operations for matte smelting <term>Stein-Schmeltzen: an intermediate smelting process that produces a concentrated sulfide called "matte" or "Stein"</term> (which is also a highly necessary task), while the lead ores are used for "work-smelting" <term>Werck-Schmeltzen: smelting lead ores to produce "work-lead," a lead-silver alloy from which pure silver is later separated</term>. In this way, they can process all their ores according to their usual customs.

*Smelter.* From what I hear, then, the Freiberg Ore Purchase is for the benefit and advancement of the
<meta>catchword: mining-</meta>

<summary>Mercurius explains that the state-run Ore Purchase system allows poor-quality ores (pyrite and galena) to be used as essential flux for smelting richer, more difficult ores, keeping marginal mines economically viable. This system supports the mining industry even when individual veins are not immediately profitable.</summary>

<header>✱ ◦ ✱</header>

...the mining companies <term>Gewerckschafften: cooperative associations of shareholders who funded mining operations</term> established, so that those works that occasionally stagnate <note>original: *ins Stecken gerathen*, literally "get stuck"</note> or otherwise haven't found rich strikes <note>original: *edle Anbrüche*, referring to the discovery of high-grade ore veins</note> yet, might recover and not be ruined in the process? This is certainly a very laudable and highly praiseworthy thing. But how do those works in the upper mountains manage, which also produce only poor ores and cannot easily bear the transport costs along with other expenses to Freiberg? Are they not also compensated <note>original: *genuget*, from *genügen*, meaning the accounts are satisfied or settled</note>?

*Mercurius.* Everything is compensated in those mountains as much as possible, and nothing is thrown away. They concentrate the ore <note>original: *in die Enge bringen*, a technical term for reducing the volume of ore by removing waste rock</note> through stamping <term>Pochen: the process of crushing ore into powder using heavy, iron-shod wooden beams in a stamp mill</term> and washing. If these still do not cover the costs of transport to Freiberg, they are smelted and processed right there at their own local smelting works, as you will find in many places there. For in almost most parts of the Ore Mountains <note>German: *Ertz-Gebürgen*</note> there are smelting and foundry works in which they formerly (before the current Ore Purchase system <note>The *Erz-Kauff* was a state-regulated system where the government purchased all mined ore at fixed prices to control the silver supply</note>) smelted their ores, and these are still used in some places for their poor-quality ores.

*Schmeltzer.* What then is the nature of the payment? You told me that the foreign mining companies are paid in cash for the silver in their ores. What value is credited there for a loth <note>A traditional unit of weight, roughly 14.6 grams or 1/32 of a civil pound</note> of silver in raw ores?

And this is the true nature and character of the Electoral Saxon Ore Purchase <term>Erzt-Kauff: the state-run system in Saxony where the sovereign held the exclusive right to buy all mined ore at fixed prices</term> in Freiberg, from which you can see that it was not established or operated until now for the profit of the high Sovereign <note>original: *Landes-Herrschafft*</note> (as you might perhaps have imagined), but rather for the benefit of the poor mining works. For the ores from the mountains <note>original: *Gebürgischen Erze*, referring to the Ore Mountains or Erzgebirge</note>, which all lack lead, and in many places also pyrites <term>Kieß: a general term for sulfide minerals, usually iron or copper pyrites</term>, are processed through this means—where these two materials are found in abundance—so that in this way, nothing is lacking for anyone.

**Smelter.** Are the rich and low-grade ores then mixed together in the charge <term>beschicket: the act of "charging" or filling a furnace with a specific ratio of ore and flux</term> and thus smelted, or how do they proceed with them?

**Mercurius.** The poor and low-grade ores are worked alone, as follows: They commonly take 112 hundredweights <term>Centner: a unit of weight, roughly 50kg</term> of various types of ore in their charge, such as pyrites, blende <note>original: *Blente*, a zinc sulfide mineral</note>, and hornstone <note>original: *Hornstein*, a silica-rich rock</note>, where the hundredweight contains only half a *Loth* <term>Loth: a historical unit of weight, approx. 14.6 grams</term> of silver; this totals 3 *Marks* <term>Marck: a unit of weight for precious metals, equal to 16 Loth</term> of silver. Furthermore, 130 hundredweights of coppery pyrites, where the hundredweight contains one *Loth* of silver, which totals 10 *Marks* and 4 *Loth* of silver. This is now a "week's work" <note>original: *Wochen-Werck*, a standardized production unit representing the amount of material processed in one week</note>, and they are charged (as required) with the fresh slag <note>original: *Frisch-Schlacken*, slag recycled from previous smelting stages to act as a flux</note> coming from the lead-work and [processed] through a blast furnace <term>hoher Ofen: literally "high furnace," referring to a tall smelting furnace</term> in one week...

...are removed from the piles, but the **work-lead** <note>the lead-silver alloy produced during smelting</note> is made to be some twenty *Loth* <term>Loth: a measure of purity; 16 Loth equaled one Mark of silver</term> in grade, and is subsequently **cupelled** <note>refined in a cupellation furnace to separate the silver from the lead</note>. Usually, for every hundred **hundredweights** <term>Centner: a unit of weight, roughly 50kg</term> of the aforementioned lead matte, fifty to sixty hundredweights of the previously mentioned nine to ten *Loth* grade raw matte are added. In a **weekly cycle** <term>Wochen-Werck: the standard operational period for a smelting team</term>, sometimes between thirty-two and two hundred and forty hundredweights of such mattes are processed together and smelted through the **blast furnace** <note>original: *hohen Ofen*</note>. This is done so that the lead mattes (as they know from experience) release the silver and lead all the sooner. In this way, they would not need to be smelted through more than three times at most, but can much sooner be set aside and made ready for **copper production** <note>the final stages of refining where copper is recovered from the remaining matte</note>.

**Smelter.** These operations in the blast furnace are thus very well performed and proceed very efficiently in this manner. But how is the furnace prepared for working with **lead glance** <term>Bley-Glantz: galena, the natural mineral form of lead sulfide</term>, so that they may properly carry out their smelting?

**Mercury.** I will teach you this very briefly and clearly, so that you may set up your work quite well. Make the **furnace lining** <term>Gestübe: a mixture of charcoal dust and clay used to line the furnace floor</term> somewhat higher around the **tuyere** <term>Forme: the nozzle where the bellows blow air into the furnace</term>, and make it steeper inside the furnace, but somewhat lower in front of the furnace. You must **ram** <term>stoffen</term> <note>stoßen: to tightly pack or compress the lining material with a tool</note> the lining well. Do not place too much at the front; it also does not require frequent clearing. The **lead ore** <note>original: *Bley-Ertz*</note> must also have an additive <note>flux</note> so that it cools and does not run too hot.

**Shale-tuff** and **flour-sand** or **water-sand** <note>fine-grained silts or sands used to help the ore melt</note> make everything fluid, as do **spar** and **red iron-ore** <note>hematite, often used as a flux</note>: however, the tuff makes the flow sluggish. Therefore, pay close attention to this distinction, for if you do not know how to properly assist your ores in the furnace, things will not go well for you, even if you possessed the richest ores. Therefore, take good note and see what your ores require, so that you may know how to respond correctly in all such matters.

**Smelter.** All that you are saying is very good; however, I believe that **lead smelting** <note>original: *Bley-Schmelzung*</note> is performed in different ways in different places: I am familiar with many such arrangements, and everyone prefers to stick to their own experience and treats the ores as the circumstances require.

**Mercurius.** For the **lead-smelting shift** <term>Bley-Schicht Arbeit: the specific work cycle and technical process for smelting lead ore</term>, prepare your furnace in this way: keep it low at the base, and close the **tap-hole** <note>original: *Auge*, literally the 'eye' of the furnace</note> with **lining** <term>Gestübe: a mixture of charcoal dust and clay used to line the furnace hearth</term> or small coals, and take your ores. If you have **matte** <term>Lech: a semi-refined mixture of metal sulfides produced during smelting</term>, add **hearth-residue** <term>Herd: lead-saturated material from the furnace floor</term> and **litharge** <term>Glätte: lead oxide, a byproduct of refining that is recycled to help recover silver</term> to it; place this individually in the middle of the furnace and let it begin to heat, followed then by your **matte**. When the **matte** rises up toward the **tuyere** <note>original: *Forme*; the nozzle through which air is blown into the furnace</note>, the furnace is tapped. This is done in **Goslar** <note>a major historic mining and smelting center in the Harz mountains</note>, where they also produce much lead and prepare their furnace in this way: when the smelter closes the furnace, he puts a few coals at the bottom in the hearth and a little **matte** above...

on top, and level it off, and pack down the **furnace lining** <term>Gestübe: a mixture of charcoal dust and clay used to line the furnace hearth</term> on top, and make a small channel in the lining, also place a small fire upon it and let it start soon; thus the lead crawls down through the lining into the coals, and the slag remains on top. **Smelter.** In Goslar <note>a famous mining town in the Harz Mountains of Germany</note>, much lead is smelted and silver is also produced; I have also been there. Even if their ores are not particularly rich, they possess them in such great quantities.

**Mercurius.** Take note: now I will teach you how to prepare a **smelting furnace** <term>Schmelz-Ofen</term> for **lustrous ore** <term>Glantz-Ertz: typically galena (lead sulfide), known for its bright, metallic shine</term>, which you will find very useful in your work. First, the furnace should have a properly "dark nose" <note>the 'nose' is the slag formation at the air inlet or tuyère; a 'dark' nose indicates the furnace is not overheating and is protecting the nozzle</note>, and you should feed the charge in lightly so that the furnace runs "darkly" <note>operating at a temperature that prevents the metal from vaporizing or burning</note>; it should also not run too easily or leak at the front wall: for what leaks from the furnace is nothing but **ore-fines** <term>Schlich: fine, crushed, and washed ore concentrate</term> and good ore. This is only a loss, and every channel in which the lead-fines are worked should be wide and usually placed before the **eye** <note>the tap-hole of the furnace</note>; furthermore, the interior of each furnace should not be too flat, but rather—

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page continues Mercurius’s technical instructions to the Smelter regarding the maintenance of the smelting furnace, specifically how to manage the "nose" of slag and the composition of the hearth lining.</meta>

<page-num>239</page-num>
<header>* o *</header>

...cleanly <note>The text begins mid-word, likely completing "saubern" (to clean or clear)</note> and fire well, and thrust quite hard; and when a furnace is built up too high, it also does not work well. If a **nose** <note>In historical smelting, a "nose" is a tube of solidified slag that forms at the end of the bellows' nozzle (tuyere). It protects the furnace wall and pre-heats the air blast.</note> falls off inside the furnace, one must place soft and fresh slags or stones; place the slags in the middle of the furnace, but the stone at the front wall, so that a nose will form again before your eyes. Also, if the smoke turns red, the ore is "clumping" <note>original: *rudelt*; refers to the ore gathering into masses instead of melting and separating</note> and does not separate in the furnace; likewise, if an ore flows out too cold, one must assist it with soft slags. And if a **furnace lining** <term>Gestübe: a mixture of charcoal dust and clay</term> has too much **clay** <term>Leim: literally "glue," here referring to the binding clay</term>, the silver and lead will burn away in the hearth, and entire pieces often tear themselves away. If it has too little clay, however, the hearth will "stand up" <note>meaning the lining becomes loose or floats up into the molten metal</note>. For every bucket full of calcined **charcoal dust** <term>Lösche: fine coal or cinders</term>, two barrows full of clay are required; this provides a good hearth for smelting **lead-glance** <term>Bley-Glanz: galena or lead sulfide ore</term>. Here I tell you, my dear Smelter, such great advantages and tricks that you would never learn from one of your own kind, even if they knew of them.

**Smelter.** It is true that these are special trade secrets in smelting, and through them I finally truly grasp the manual labor, which I did not understand so well before; sometimes I did not know what the cause was when everything did not go according to my wish and will. I can never repay you for this good deed.

**Mercurius.** Now I will teach you some more trade secrets which will serve <note>The text ends here; the catchword "dienen" (to serve) indicates the start of the next page.</note> you in your work not a little.

...will serve [to show] how one person can cause another particular harm and mischief in the **smelting works** <term>Schmeltz-Hütten: the industrial buildings where ore is processed</term>, so that often the one who does not rightly understand his craft thinks he is bewitched. First, mischief happens to you if someone shifts your **bellows** <term>Balgen</term>, and second, if someone tampers with the **rocker arm** <term>Schwengel: the lever that operates the bellows</term>, the **wedges** <term>Keilen</term>, or the **cam** <term>Kamm: the piece on the water-wheel shaft that trips the bellows</term>, or if they clear [the furnace] too high or too low with the **tapping irons** <term>Stech-Eisen: long iron rods used to poke or clear the furnace openings</term>. All of this you can recognize by the furnace when it works poorly for you, such as heating too much at the top and not at the bottom, and the like. O, you must diligently seek out such defects.

**Smelter.** Certainly, it often happens that the work of smelting goes so confusedly that one does not know the cause, nor how to help oneself, which is then often due to such tricks and practical jokes; and so one must take care in many ways if one wishes to bring his work to a successful conclusion.

**Mercurius.** Now I will further tell you how they make their silver and copper in the **Inntal** <note>original: *Ihmthal*; the Inn Valley in the Alps, a region historically famous for its mining and smelting operations</note>: for much copper is smelted there, which is performed in the following manner. When the smelter there seals [the furnace], he lays a large **block** <term>Knittel: a piece of wood or a thick stick</term> under the **eye** <term>Auge: the tap-hole of the furnace</term>, then he lets half of it go into the furnace; where it comes out below into the channel inside the furnace the block is thick, and at the front of the furnace it is small, when he...

...has closed it, so that he cuts and has already cut the **tap channel** <term>Spuhr: the groove through which molten metal flows</term>, he then knocks the **plug** <term>Knittel: a wooden stick or plug used to stop the furnace tap-hole</term> back out of the furnace and prepares the hearth. He lets the **slimes** <term>Schlich: finely crushed or washed ore concentrates</term> proceed and adds the **lead ore** to the slimes. Once he has the hearth full, he taps it from the side and extracts all the silver from the **copper matte** <term>Kupffer-Stein: an intermediate product in copper smelting containing copper, iron, and sulfur</term>. He then places the lead and the copper onto the **liquation furnace** <term>Seiger-Ofen: a furnace used to separate silver-rich lead from copper by heating them to a specific melting point</term>; the lead then runs off into the hearth during the smelting and carries the silver with it. This [silver-lead alloy] is subsequently **refined** <term>abtreiben: the process of cupellation to separate silver from lead</term>. One then **roasts** <term>dörret: literally "dries," referring to the roasting process to remove remaining impurities</term> the copper more thoroughly, lets it go through the furnace, and finishes it, as you well know. They usually have a **liquation hearth** <term>Seiger-Herd</term> right there in their works; thus, they extract all the silver from the copper themselves so that they do not have to sell it to any [external] liquation works. They also have vaults over their smelting furnaces in which they capture the smoke; whatever settles there, they turn back into copper and silver <note>This refers to the recovery of metal-rich flue dust, an early form of industrial recycling</note>.

***Smelter.*** Much is to be learned from these accounts of smelting, regarding how they are accustomed to performing them in various places. I have also heard much about the **shales** <term>Schiefern: copper-bearing bituminous shale</term> of **Eisleben** and **Mansfeld**, that they are said to produce much copper from them there.

***Mercurius.*** The well-known copper-bearing **shale** varieties in the County of **Mansfeld** and **Eisleben**—

...**pigments** <note>original: "Farben"</note>, they are indeed not as rich in copper and silver, yet because of the great quantity in which they occur, they are still profitable to use; although there they only roast and charge them in the common manner, as I will also show you, since these currently standard and common charging and smelting processes at all smelting works are very well known to me, even if in many places they still want to act so secretively about them. These aforementioned **shales** <term>Schiefer: a fine-grained sedimentary rock, here specifically the copper-bearing shale of central Germany</term> are now melted through the **blast furnaces** <term>hohe Ofen: tall furnaces used for continuous smelting</term> into **matte** <term>Stein: an intermediate product in copper smelting containing copper, iron, and sulfur</term>, which is however very low in copper, since they often must use some fifty **hundredweight** <term>Centner: a unit of weight, roughly 110 pounds</term> of such shale to produce one hundredweight of **refined copper** <term>Gar Kupffer: finished, high-purity copper</term>; yet a hundredweight of such copper contains one **mark** <term>Marck: a unit of weight for precious metals, approx. 233 grams</term>, and sometimes one and a half [marks] of silver. These copper-bearing shales are first roasted in the common way, and they place a hundred or even two hundred hundredweight at once into such a **roasting pile** <term>Roſt: a heap where ore is heated to remove sulfur</term>; underneath they lay bundles of **brushwood** <term>Reiſſig</term>, light the pile, and let it burn. In smelting, they add nothing but the shale and only a little **flux** <term>Fluß: a material added to promote melting and the separation of slag</term>, and let it stand day and night; from this is produced their copper matte, which they then roast five to six times, and then turn into **black copper** <term>schwartz Kupffer: unrefined, crude copper</term>, which they then bring to completion after **liquation** <term>Seigerung: the process of separating silver from copper by using the different melting points of lead and copper</term>.

**Smelter.** Are the Eisleben and Mansfeld shales so poor in copper? I would not have imagined that. I have certainly never tested any of them, but I have seen them in very many places, where they, because of their wondrous **im-** <note>original: "Bild-", likely completing as "Bildnisse" (images/fossils) or "Bildung" (formation) on the next page</note>

**Smelter.** I see from this that these shales have various charges, depending on how they are processed: for I well believe that they have performed many kinds of experiments there, and tried many a charge to enrich their content; similar shale ores are also found around **Saalfeld** <note>A historic mining town in Thuringia, Germany</note>, where they also make copper and silver from them.

**Mercurius.** The **Saalfeld copper ores and shales** <note>Saalfeld, in Thuringia, was a significant mining center known for its alum and copper production.</note> are very volatile, and in and of themselves they possess little value; nonetheless, smelting continues there constantly. They have **vaulted** all the furnaces in their smelting works so that they might capture the smoke; they sweep out these vaults from time to time (about once every quarter) after heavy smelting has occurred, and from this smoke they produce **copper and silver** <note>This refers to "flue dust," which contains fine metallic particles carried upward by the draft and deposited on the walls of the chimneys.</note>. These vaults are connected to one another above the furnaces so that the smoke can drift back and forth until it must finally settle and be caught, since it cannot escape further upwards. The furnaces are also high at the front, with an iron "neck" <term>Nacken: likely an arched opening or hood</term> shaped in a semi-circle, clad with sheet metal and closed up. In front of this stands a **tapping-trace** <term>Spur: a basin or channel where molten metal collects</term> or crucible, and next to it on the side is another trace which stands slightly lower. Thus, one taps into the upper crucible or trace, and the material then flows into the lower one and becomes **matte** <term>Stein: a mixture of metal and sulfur that is an intermediate product in smelting</term>. From this, the matte is then lifted off and broken into discs <note>original: "zu Scheiben gerissen"</note>.

When the smelter prepares the **charge** <term>beschicket: the specific recipe of materials fed into the furnace</term> there, he proceeds as follows: he takes 15 carts of the ore or shale he wish to smelt (which contain approximately 1/2 **Lot** <term>Loth: approx. 14.6 grams</term> of silver per **hundredweight** <term>Centner: approx. 110 lbs</term>) and adds 3 hundredweight of **pyrite** <note>original: "Kieß"</note> that contains no silver. This yields 2 hundredweight of matte, which contains approximately 10 Lots of silver per hundredweight. Once he has 36 hundredweight of matte, it is then processed using lead; for this, 5 hundredweight of **hearth-material** <term>Herd: the saturated lining of a furnace, often containing residual metals</term>, 5 hundredweight of **litharge** <term>Glätt: lead oxide, a byproduct of silver refining</term>, 2 hundredweight of fresh lead, and **slag** <note>original: "Schlacken"</note> are melted with it, as the circumstances of the work require; he takes...

the **matte** <term>Stein: an intermediate product of smelting containing metal and sulfur</term> again and adds to it 4 **hundredweights** <term>Centner: a unit of weight, roughly 100-110 pounds</term> of **hearth-lead** <note>original: "Herd" – refers to the lead-rich residues or saturated lining of the furnace hearth used as a flux</note>, 4 hundredweights of **litharge** <term>Glött: lead monoxide (PbO), used to separate silver from copper</term>, and produces **copper matte**. **Smelter.** I hear from all these accounts of yours that in no single place is **Nature** truly followed in the roasting and smelting of ores, and that your **secret operations** are not yet known to them at all—even though, without any doubt, the greatest profit is created through such things, and a simple person could grasp it very well and easily if it were only explained to them clearly.

**Mercurius.** In our beloved **Germany**, this high science just explained (I mean the proper recognition of Nature’s working) is still a hidden secret. All the true concepts once held regarding it have died out along with those who understood them, so that we now possess nothing other than false and self-invented, destructive errors instead of the true foundation. Because of this, the well-known proverb has arisen among foreigners that the **Germans are blind**, and in na-

...cannot see in natural things, which means nothing other than that, although they possess and own the best and richest gold and silver ores in their own lands (which many other lands and kingdoms in the world lack), they nonetheless neither use nor employ them. This is despite the fact that everyone there is occupied with very burdensome labors and tasks to gain wealth; yet the path that could lead and guide them to it with a good conscience and less effort, they do not see or find. This proverb certainly holds perfectly true in these current times, and in most places where one searches in the earth for such things, daily experience confirms it.

*Smelter.* I have heard of this proverb, in which it is said that the Italians see with both eyes, the Nurembergers <note>Nuremberg was a center of precision engineering and commerce, often seen as a middle ground between Italian theory and German labor.</note> with one eye, but the Germans do not see at all; from which I have concluded that in Italy, there must be men experienced in Nature who distinguish themselves above all Germans in the investigation of the **mineral kingdom**, and to whom the rare and hidden sciences of Nature are no longer a particular secret. By these means, they are also able to carry away most of the gold and precious stones from our German lands into their own, of which all the mountains can sing and tell. <note>A reference to the "Walen" or "Venetians," legendary Italian prospectors rumored to have secretly extracted gold from the German Alps and Harz mountains.</note>

**Smelter.** The Italians also have a proverb: "The German throws a stone at a cow, even though the stone is often worth more than the cow." <note>This proverb illustrates the perceived ignorance of German laborers who, unaware of mineralogy, might toss aside a valuable ore as if it were a common fieldstone.</note> Thus, they must ultimately know our mountains very well, and know quite well where the richest gold ores and noblest stones must be hidden. *Mercurius.* In these current times, such people are as rare in Nuremberg as in other places; and just as this city belongs to Germany, they also lack such sciences <note>In this context, "sciences" refers to the practical and "secret" knowledge of metallurgy and alchemy.</note>. How or in what manner this well-known proverb arose, and what the deal is with the "Nuremberg Ore-Purchase" <term>Ore-Purchase: original "Ertz-Kauff," referring to a historical period in Nuremberg where citizens speculated on raw minerals as if they were currency</term>, I will thoroughly explain to you: In olden times, various foreign artists (especially Italians) stayed there. These men not only collected special gold and silver specimens <note>original: "Stuffen," a mining term for high-quality mineral samples</note> from the rich ore-mountains, but also traded for them from whoever was willing to bring them there. For such rich ores, grains, and marcasites <term>marcasites: iron sulfide minerals; here used to describe various metallic-looking stones that might contain hidden precious metals</term>, they would pay a hundred or more Thalers <note>Thaler: a large silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries; the ancestor of the word "dollar"</note> per pound, based on their internal content, especially if the minerals were quite native <note>original: "gediegen," meaning the metal is found in nature in its pure metallic form rather than as a chemical compound</note>. These artists made a great profit from these rich and native gold ores because they possessed the art of smelting such (albeit volatile) types into stable and fixed gold <note>In alchemical theory, "volatile" gold was seen as unstable or immature, while "fixed" gold was permanent and pure</note>. Now, when those who were specifically acquainted with such artists saw that they obtained such a rich benefit from it, they also took up this "Ore-Purchase." They bought up such ores, garnets, grains, and marcasites in great quantities, believing that (since so much gold was hidden within them) it was as useful and good as ready money. However, they did not concern themselves any further with the smelting of these minerals, but rather kept them, so to speak, as their treasure. But when, afterward, at a quite...
