<page-num>250</page-num>
<header>* . *</header>

...nets, <term>marcasites</term> <note>original: "Marcasitten"; in this era, the term referred generally to crystallized metal sulfides like pyrite, which were thought to be "unripe" gold</note>, and grains in whole barrels and chest-fulls, which no one there is able to use anymore. You could buy them back for a meager price; however, you will find very few buyers for the goods you brought with you, unless you were simply to give them away.

**Smelter.** So, have the people of Nuremberg been completely deceived by such <term>volatile</term> <note>original: "flüchtigen"; referring to substances that evaporate or "fly away" when heated, making them difficult to smelt without specialized alchemical knowledge</note> gold ores, garnets, grains, and marcasites? They purchased a considerable quantity of them in order to gain a great profit, only to lose their investment so shamefully when they expected to make a good return? But could they not have sold such things back to their known <term>artists</term> <note>original: "Künstler"; refers to master metallurgists or alchemists who possessed secret techniques for refining difficult ores</note> at that time? If they had, they would have lost nothing.

**Mercurius.** My good man, when those artists were still alive, such ores, garnets, and marcasites were not available for resale to them, for those people held such materials higher than gold itself at that time. Because they knew for certain that rich gold was present within them, they were content with everything and bought away boldly, whatever was brought to them. They did not concern themselves at all with the method of how to smelt and <term>refine</term> <note>original: "zu gute machen"; a technical term for processing ore to extract the valuable metal</note> them—viewing it as a trivial matter—because it went so well for the true artists. When the people saw that the artists could smelt the finest gold from these materials, they were under the impression that anyone could do it. However, this later proved to be quite the opposite, as was shown and demonstrated. From this also...

<meta>catchword: just</meta>

<summary>Mercurius explains that the people of Nuremberg lost money on gold-bearing minerals because they assumed smelting was easy, only to find the skill died with the old "artists." The page highlights the difference between possessing raw materials and possessing the technical knowledge to process them.</summary>

<keywords>metallurgy, gold refining, marcasite, Nuremberg, alchemy, smelting, economic loss, volatile ores, technical expertise</keywords>

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Mercurius and the Smelter regarding the decline of technical knowledge in Nuremberg. Mercurius concludes his point about the loss of expertise before the Smelter expresses relief at being spared a fruitless journey.</meta>

->* o *<-

...hence this proverb arose: that the people of Nuremberg at least see with one eye (and thus are said to be even cleverer in these matters than those in other places), while the rest of the Germans were completely blind. <note>The proverb "In the land of the blind, the one-eyed man is king" is applied here to Nuremberg’s historical reputation as a center of superior mechanical and metallurgical skill during the Renaissance.</note> However, in this science, they have now completely lost their eye as well, and are thus just as blind as those in other places.

**Smelter.** Well certainly, if you had not come to me so unexpectedly today and revealed this to me along with such secret and rare sciences, I would have set off very soon with a knapsack full of such gold ores, garnets, and <term>marcasites</term> <note>A term used historically for various shiny mineral sulfides like pyrite; they were often mistaken for or thought to contain "unripe" gold.</note> in order to earn a sum of money with them according to my prescribed instructions. My journey would thus have been in vain and for nothing; that would have truly vexed me, to have walked such a long way for nothing, though I would not have been able to help myself. I intended to go to the <term>Mint</term> there and have a trial made by the local <term>Warden</term> <note>original: "Guardein"; the Assay Master or official responsible for testing the purity of precious metals at a mint.</note> (since such a person would best know how to handle them), so that I could have set my price accordingly. But I realize now that I would likely have met with failure there as well. So, it is indeed good that you have spared me this great effort and troublesome journey. But now, I no longer require such efforts, because I intend to take the matter into my own hands and, according to the instruction you have given, diligently practice such <term>figuring</term> <note>original: "Figirung"; an alchemical term referring to the process of "fixing" or giving a stable physical form to volatile metallic spirits so they do not evaporate during heating.</note> myself; then I shall have no need to trouble myself further with others,

<summary>Mercurius explains that Nuremberg has lost its legendary metallurgical expertise, becoming as "blind" as the rest of Germany. The Smelter expresses relief that he did not waste a journey to the Nuremberg Mint, resolving instead to practice the alchemical "figuring" of ores himself.</summary>
<keywords>Nuremberg, metallurgy, Assay Master, Mint, gold ore, marcasites, alchemical fixation, figuring</keywords>

--- Page 282 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Mercurius and the Smelter. The Smelter reflects on how Mercurius's instructions have saved him from a useless journey and given him the confidence to process his own ores rather than seeking help from the failing experts in Nuremberg.</meta>

->* ○ *<-

...[and] still to worry about who might want to buy such a thing from me; I already know how to handle <term>roasting</term> and <term>smelting</term>, <note>Roasting involves heating ore to drive off volatile impurities like sulfur; smelting is the subsequent melting of the ore to extract the metal.</note> and if I only take your teachings as my guide, I hope, alongside **GOtt**, <note>original: "GOtt" (God); the Smelter acknowledges divine aid as a prerequisite for success in the "Great Work."</note> that I shall not lack for a successful outcome.

*Mercurius.* One who is instructed as I have instructed you will surely succeed; if such a person should fail, the fault would lie within themselves. For if someone thought that everything should simply fly together at once for him, without any further effort, he would be mistaken. Without toil and labor, no one has anything, as everyone well knows. For after many investigations of nature, one finally reaches the true goal, and then that small amount of effort will seem like nothing at all in light of the magnificent reward.

*Schmelzer.* I believe all of this very well, my dear Mercurius, for I can now thoroughly oversee the beginning and end of this entire art. I also recognize quite well what the master <note>In an alchemical context, the "master" or "agent" is the active force or catalyst that directs the transformation of the matter.</note> is in such works, and how everything must be carried out according to the required circumstances through natural ways. Therefore, no more doubt remains in me that could make me suspicious of the hoped-for result, because I have now become quite a different man—and almost a half-philosopher—who yields himself entirely to the providence of God.

<summary>The Smelter expresses confidence in his new metallurgical knowledge, while Mercurius emphasizes that alchemical success requires diligent work rather than instant results. The Smelter concludes that he is now a changed man, having adopted the mindset of a "philosopher" or student of nature's secrets.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, roasting, smelting, labor and reward, divine providence, alchemical philosophy, transformation</keywords>

--- Page 283 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Mercurius and the Smelter. On the previous page, the Smelter expressed his newfound confidence, claiming he had become "almost a half-philosopher" thanks to Mercurius’s guidance.</meta>

-> * ∘ * <-

**Mercurius.** I assure you, my dear Smelter, if you weigh everything well and truly take to heart what I have now quite sincerely and faithfully taught you, that you must no longer be such a foolish fellow <note>original: "tummer Teufel" - literally "dumb devil," a colloquialism for a fool or simpleton.</note> who would not know what was black or white, and could not fathom from this instruction what is in accordance with nature or against it. Furthermore, you now know the difference between the right, true art and the false art of <term>roasting and smelting</term>, <note>In alchemy and early metallurgy, "roasting" (heating to remove sulfur) and "smelting" (melting to extract metal) were seen as processes that could either perfect or destroy the "soul" of the metal.</note> and that your profits can be no different than what your practiced labors bring with them. Would you now, as a sensible man, dare to set yourself against these works I have specified, and bring your wretched and world-weary <term>slop-work</term> <note>original: "Sudel-Arbeit"; refers to unskilled, messy, or unscientific metallurgical practice often criticized by alchemical masters.</note> to market against my nature-aligned science of roasting and smelting? What do you think? Would not everyone laugh at you as soon as you only made the trial, because with such a useless method not the slightest effect of any improvement is to be hoped for? But as soon as you find some true tracks following Nature’s paths, then abandon the old junk immediately and follow that which leads you to it: for once you have reached that point, you will not be able to go astray again.

**Schmelzer.** Because I now understand it better, and know how to arrange my affairs quite differently, I shall no longer dare to offend my conscience by seeking out those old and destructive <term>fire-works</term> <note>original: "Feuer-Arbeiten"; here referring to harsh, high-heat metallurgical processes that "destroy" the subtle qualities of the metal rather than perfecting or "ripening" it according to alchemical principles.</note> again, but shall henceforth at all times apply myself to [following] nature in everything...
bens <note>This is a catchword, likely the beginning of "lebens" (life) on the following page.</note>

<summary>Mercurius challenges the Smelter to use his new knowledge to distinguish between "natural" metallurgy and the "slop-work" of unskilled laborers. The Smelter vows to abandon his former destructive methods and follow the path of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Mercurius, Smelter, metallurgy, roasting and smelting, nature, slop-work, fire-works, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between Mercurius and the Smelter. On the previous page, the Smelter was beginning to explain how he would now arrange his work differently, based on the natural principles he has learned.</meta>

-> * ∘ * <-

...provide the necessary guidance, so that it <note>Referring to the "nature" of the smelting process discussed previously.</note> may perform and accomplish its usual effect without hindrance, and gladden us with rich yields <note>original: "Ausbeuthen" – a term specifically used for the profit or ore extracted from a mine.</note>.

***Mercurius.*** Now I shall take my leave of you again, for I have rested quite enough, and I will see how things are progressing elsewhere. Meanwhile, I commend you to the goodness of your Creator, under whose faithful providence you may conduct everything wisely. Do not abandon my faithful teaching, and proceed in all things as I have told you; you will be rewarded with rich profit and be capable of achieving that which almost the whole world now considers impossible. Use such sciences <term>Wissenschaften</term> <note>In the 18th century, "science" referred to any systematic art or technical knowledge, specifically the "art" of alchemy and metallurgy in this context.</note> for your benefit, and do not forget to do good for the poor; thus, in this temporal life, you shall not have to worry about finding abundant sustenance, and you will find everywhere a rich
->Good luck!<- <note>original: "Glück auf" – the traditional German miners' greeting, literally wishing for the ore veins to "open up" or for a safe return to the surface.</note>
